| Rome — Via Sacra Scholarly District | RME | The city of Rome in the late medieval period: the Via Sacra with its classical ruins and humanist scholars' houses, a scholars' quarter near the old forum where Latin and Greek learning exist in proximity, the river road running north from Rome toward the Alps; the city where Greek documents from the eastern Mediterranean tradition meet the Latin scholarly world; a waystation on the route from the Mediterranean to northern European archives | Classical scholarship, the translation between Greek and Latin traditions, the scholar who wants to read before the archive has the item, the road north as the final leg before Weimar |
| Rome — Civic Archive and South Gate District | ROM | Rome in 1367: the civic archive in the old forum district where legal documents are held in restricted custody; the south gate leading to the Appian Way and Campania; the road that runs north toward Bologna and the Via Emilia; the smell of old parchment and legal dust and the specific atmosphere of a city whose archives are still the repository for active legal instruments; avvocati with injunctions waiting at the gate; the first checkpoint on the northbound route | The live legal instrument in archival custody, the procedural injunction as the gate obstacle, the distinction between historical document and active evidence, the road north where jurisdiction transitions from Roman to Bolognese and then to the archive's own |
| Constantinople — Scholar's Private Library | CPL | Private library of a Byzantine theologian and diplomat in Constantinople, 1367: stacked manuscripts in cedar shelves, Latin and Greek volumes mixed, a window overlooking the harbor where Genoese galleys are moored; the kind of library assembled by a man who understands it will not last | Classical manuscript custody, the private scholarly collection preceding the institutional one, the library assembled in full knowledge of its fragility |
| Black Sea Coastal Road | BLK | The trade and pilgrim route along the Anatolian Black Sea coast from the Bosphorus east to Trebizond: caravanserais one day's ride apart, Genoese and Venetian agents at every harbor stop, mountain brigands on the inland passes; the route the army used, in the other direction, seventeen hundred years ago | Road transit, the ambush point, the Genoese inspection gate, the route the army also traveled |
| Sinope — Black Sea Harbor City | SIN | A Pontic harbor city under Genoese commercial influence in 1367, on the northern Anatolian coast: the merchant quarter near the waterfront, the smell of salted fish and rope; the old Greek-speaking community intact from ancient settlement; cedar chests in back rooms with documents that have not been read in decades | Classical document custody in a merchant family, the harbor city between Greek tradition and Genoese commerce, the collector who learned what he owned from someone else |
| Orchomenos — Boeotian Monastery Library | ORC | The monastery library at Orchomenos in Boeotia, in the hills above Lake Kopais, 1367: an autumn library with its archive chest; the Florentine Duke of Athens claims jurisdiction over classical manuscripts found in Boeotia; a scholar from the Mystras academy published the find three months ago; Brother Stavros who catalogued it is careful and cooperative | Classical manuscript discovery, the prior publication claim, Florentine jurisdiction over Boeotian finds, the monastic archivist who found something he did not fully understand |
| Varna — Black Sea Civic Archive | VAR | The Varna civic archive on the western Black Sea coast, 1367: the head clerk Radu processes all inquiries in order; a practical city archive in a commercial harbor; Genoese purchase inquiries processed alongside scholarly commissions; the distinction between custodial authority and commercial transaction is not obvious to Radu | Civic archive procedure, the distinction between purchase inquiry and archival commission, practical custodial bureaucracy |
| Mystras — Peloponnesian Scholarly City | MYS | Mystras in 1367, the Despotate of the Morea: a monastery recently relocated here; the Mystras academy where Byzantine scholars study classical texts; Father Demetrios who has waited three years for the right archive destination; the city where classical scholarship is being preserved at the edge of the Byzantine world | Classical scholarship at the Byzantine periphery, the religious archivist choosing between publication and preservation, the city where waiting has been the right choice |
| Ancona — Adriatic Port City | ANC | The Italian port of Ancona on the Adriatic, 1367: the harbor where Byzantine and Italian shipping meet; the Papal legation office; the transit point between Greece and the Italian peninsula; the city where documents from the Greek world enter the Latin scholarly network | Adriatic transit, the crossing from Byzantine to Italian jurisdiction, the first Italian archive contact point for documents traveling from the eastern Mediterranean |
| Sant'Urbano alla Caffarella — Appian Way Monastery | SAU | A small monastery near the Appian Way south of Rome, 1367: ancient stone walls, a secondary archive room with the smell of dry stone and cedar; Urban V's reinventory teams are working the road nearby; a place where objects have been sleeping in unlabeled boxes since the fourth century and are about to be reached; the monks know they have things but not what things | The archive sleeping under the road official's authority, the forty-year wait for a carrier, the object extracted before the papal inventory claims it |
| Via Flaminia Apennine Section | VFM | The mountain road through the Apennine passes north of Rome: narrow stone road, cliff on one side, rock face on the other at the high points; river fog rising from the Po valley below; autumn damp on the north-facing slopes; the pass where travelers can be timed and intercepted; the place where the road narrows to one person wide | The interception point, the ambush on the narrow pass, the road that commits the traveler to a single line of movement before the valley opens |
| Bologna — Humanist Quarter | BLO | A humanist scholar's house and study in Bologna, 1367: manuscript copies on every surface, a lamp burning late into the night, the smell of new ink and old books; distinct from the official university and ecclesiastical archive district; the place where good ideas about preservation meet the practical question of who controls what gets copied | The scholarly intermediary who wants to annotate rather than release, the good-faith academic claim that conflicts with archival permanence, the copy made before the original moves |
| Troy Harbor — Greek Fleet Loading | TRH | The harbor below the blackened walls in the days after the sack: Greek ships being provisioned and loaded, Trojan women in lines at the harbor registry being assigned to Greek households, the smell of smoke still in the air and the sound of the sea going on regardless; Aethra at the edge of the line with a cedar sleeve under her arm, turning away before the conversation can have time to become grief; the checkpoint officer with his property log and the specific authority that turns a publicly delivered funeral speech into a husband's chattel | The account from inside the city; the cause of the war filing a record of what it cost; the voice that will be absorbed into the Greek version unless it leaves today under its own name |
| Scaean Gate Road (Ruins) | SKN | The road through what was the Scaean Gate district: one column-base still standing, the gate-arch fallen and passable, the road beneath it still serviceable under the rubble; the view down to the plain from the gate-gap where Andromache stood and watched and Priam leaned on the battlements; now a checkpoint, now a salvage zone, now a place where Greek captains argue about what belongs to whom | The threshold of a city that no longer exists, the geography of a farewell site, contested ownership of ruins |
| Pilgrim Keeper's Quarter | PKR | A dusty district at the gate of a garrison city on the pilgrimage road from the east: hospices in converted merchant houses, the smell of dried sage and tallow and old leather and the specific grief of men who have outlived what they were made for; the third room of the long hospice has a window onto the outer wall and a bed where a very old man lies with one hand on a cloth pouch | Sacred commission, the carrier who cannot put the charge down, the deathbed transfer, the thing that is both inheritance and debt |
| St. George Shrine Town | SGA | A small walled town in the limestone hills: one long street from the eastern gate to the shrine compound, the shrine building itself low-roofed and old, the donation registry office in a side room with two centuries of sealed votive boxes on stone shelves; a toll gate at the main entrance; a secondary pilgrims' passage in the eastern wall, low, unregistered, known to regular devotees | Sacred contract, the donation register, the thirty-year-old entry marked "awaited," the keeper who receives what arrives without asking why it took so long |
| Olindo and Sophronia's Quarter, Jerusalem | OLN | A small house in the old Christian quarter of Jerusalem after the fall: a scribe's desk by the window, the smell of old stone and olive oil; Olindo's careful handwriting on the deposition pages and his instinct that the record must be corrected even if Sophronia does not share it; Sophronia at the window in the afternoon, composed as she has been since the stake, deciding something with the stillness of a woman who survived her own death and has been sorting through what that means ever since | The false confession that saved everyone and is now a crime in the record; the deposition that cannot be filed without the signature of the person whose lie it describes; the threshold of self-exposure as an act of completion |
| Jerusalem Administrative Archive | JAR | A commandeered Saracen administrative building in Jerusalem's city center, now operating under Christian administration: Alexios the Greek clerk at the intake desk, the permanent record ledger open, two centuries of city records on the stone shelves; the smell of old paper and the specific quality of a room that has served every government that held the city and will serve the next one; the third bell audible from the window | The conviction record and the supplementing deposition filed side by side; the archive that does not adjudicate but holds both; the notation that says: both persist |
| Naples Scriptorium | NSM | Ruined Franciscan scriptorium near old Cumae, southern Italy: the roof fell in 1358, the stone table remains, salt air from the Bay of Naples below, cedar-box smell in the surviving floor alcoves; the place where the Mural Brief slept for 2,100 years before Brother Eudo found it | Ancient custody, the document too old for any institution to claim, the keeper who cannot complete the journey himself, the archive's last mile |
| Alpine Transit Gate | APT | Waystation monastery of San Agostino at the Alpine approach north of Lombardy: thick stone walls, a refectory that serves twelve travelers at a time, the bishop's road agent already at table when you arrive, a mountain pass visible through the high window; the institutional threshold between Italy and the Empire | The road gate between Mediterranean origins and northern archives, the bishop's reach extending past the Alps, the contested document at altitude |
| Montbrione Monastery | MBR | A Sicilian monastery being dissolved by papal order: half its brothers already transferred, the prior's study stacked with crated records, the chapter house floor lifted in sections where the old floor-stone repository is being inventoried; Brother Tommaso, last remaining monk, tending the last candle in the chapel; the smell of lime-dust and old beeswax and a dissolution that has turned every room into an argument about what is still canonical and what is already history | Institutional dissolution, the document without legal status, the last keeper before the official erasure, the fifty-year secret that has outlasted everyone who made it |
| Palermo Archive District | PAR | A district of walled archive buildings in a southern Italian port city: three competing institutions within four streets — papal notarial office, royal registry, city registrar — each with its own intake hours and jurisdictional claims; a harbor road running through the district that gives access to the main city, currently blocked by a warehouse fire three streets down; the smell of sea-air and smoke and old parchment and competing sealing wax | Competing jurisdictions, restricted intake, the document that must exist in two registers to be secure, institutional authority at its most granular |

| Nishapur — Madrasa Scholar Quarter | NIS | Eastern Khorasan city, 1367 under fragmenting post-Ilkhanid/Muzaffarid authority: mud-brick madrasa buildings around a tiled courtyard, the smell of old paper and dust, a main bazaar road running east-west through the city, scholars from many traditions in residence; the city that produced Omar Khayyam and still carries that weight; the feeling of a place at the edge of an empire that is about to collapse | Eastern manuscript custody, the scholar who acquired what was not his, the thing in transit that has no obvious owner, scholarly exemption versus customs authority |
| Ganja — Armenian Monastery and Scholar Quarter | GNJ | Caucasian city in Azerbaijan, 1367, under Jalayirid nominal authority with de facto Armenian community self-governance in the monastery and scholarly quarter: thick-walled monastery library with manuscripts in Armenian, Arabic, and Persian, a scholar community that survived the Mongol period by virtue of the library's isolation and the respect of successive authorities for its medical functions; the smell of old stone and tallow and cedarwood; a city at the edge of where Arabic, Persian, Turkish, and Armenian scholarly traditions physically overlap | Monastery library custody, cross-traditional scholarly translation, the text that crosses between religious and practical categories, the monastery keeper's theological reading |
| Maragha — Observatory District and Road Junction Inn Quarter | MRG | Northwestern Iranian city in Azerbaijan province, 1367, under Jalayirid authority: famous as the site of the Maragha Observatory built under Hulagu Khan (1259), still a center of astronomical study; a city of scholars, instrument-makers, and road junction traders; the inn quarter near the observatory district serves the Tabriz-Isfahan road; the smell of saffron, charcoal smoke, and the quality of a city where precision instruments are made alongside commercial traffic; travelers stop here because it is the junction, not the destination | Road junction recovery, the traveler with something that does not belong to them, astronomical scholarship as context, the object that changed hands before it arrived |
| Bursa — Ottoman Silk Market and Merchant District | BRS | Ottoman Anatolia's former capital city, 1367, under Murad I (court relocated to Adrianople but Bursa retains administrative and commercial prominence): the silk market district with caravanserais and merchant warehouses, the Byzantine walls still standing around the old city, the covered bazaar near the Ulu Cami site, the smell of raw silk and horse and cedar storage; a city in transition between Byzantine heritage and Ottoman expansion, where Armenian, Greek, and Turkish merchant communities coexist under Ottoman commercial law | Long-distance luxury goods delivery, the collector who reconsiders, Ottoman commercial property, the transition point between Eastern manuscripts and Western archive routes |
| Samarkand — Scholar and Merchant Quarter | SAM | Central Asian Silk Road junction city, 1367 under Chagatai Khanate authority (Timur consolidating control nearby): blue-tiled architecture, caravanserais serving trade from China to the Mediterranean, scholars and translators from a dozen traditions; a city where Eastern and Western intellectual materials cross regularly and unusually; the smell of horse and dye-vat and cedar chest and old ink | Silk Road transit, translation across traditions, the manuscript far from home, pedagogical materials as cultural evidence |
| Merv — Administrative and Scholar District | MRV | Ancient Silk Road city in the Merv Oasis, eastern Khorasan, 1367: still substantial despite Mongol devastation a century prior; a city of libraries and the ruins of libraries; scholars who understand that everything here exists because it survived something; the smell of dust and irrigation-water and old binding | The city that survived destruction, the library as survival strategy, comparative scholarship as response to loss, materials that traveled too far to return |
| Constantinople — Byzantine Imperial Scholar Quarter | CON | The Byzantine capital in 1367, under John V Palaiologos: a city that knows it is surrounded and carries itself with the specific dignity of that knowledge; great stone walls, sea on three sides, the Hagia Sophia visible from every high point, scholars working in the shadow of what they are trying to preserve; the smell of incense and sea-salt and old parchment and the particular quality of light in a city that was the center of the world and is learning to be something else | Imperial preservation, the archive as act of defiance, handwriting as legal testimony, comparative scholarship across traditions at the last major Byzantine institution |
| Chios — Genoese Harbor and Notary District | CHI | Aegean island under the Genoese Maona consortium from 1346; harbor district with chandlers, notaries, and ship chandleries; the Genoese administration building above the harbor; the smell of mastic and salt and ink; an island that knows it is a commercial asset, not a homeland, and carries itself accordingly | Commercial dispute, notarial authority, the document at the margin of two jurisdictions, Genoese-Venetian competition in the same harbor |
| Rhodes — Hospitallers Commandery and Harbor | RHD | The Knights of St. John's island headquarters from 1309; a fortified harbor town with the commandery above the port, the Grand Prior's session hall, the harbor master's inspection post, and the competing consulates of Genoa and Venice watching each other from adjacent streets; the smell of siege provisions, sea salt, and old stone; an island organized entirely around the exercise of organized military-religious authority at the border of Christendom and the Levant | Adjudication, the corsair/pirate distinction, competing jurisdictions, the Prior's pen as the instrument of legal classification |
| Caffa — Genoese Black Sea Colony | CAF | Genoese-administered trading post on the Crimean coast; a walled town at the edge of the Mongol world; the smell of fish, felt, horse, and Genoese counting-house ink; the point where the Silk Road meets the Black Sea; in 1347 this was the entry point of the Black Death into Europe; in 1367 still operating as the furthest reliable Genoese outpost | Commission registration, the extreme-range document, Mongol-Genoese legal interface, the letter of marque at the edge of European jurisdiction |
| Famagusta — Cypriot Harbor, Lusignan Kingdom | FAM | The principal port of Cyprus under Lusignan rule, 1367; one of the wealthiest trading cities in the eastern Mediterranean; the smell of spices, silk, and salt; competing Genoese and Venetian quarters; a cathedral begun in French Gothic style; the transit point for Crusade traffic that mostly doesn't happen anymore; the city where everything passes through and nothing stays | Transit, the document in passage between two worlds, ransom exchange, the place where the corsair and the merchant meet in a controlled setting |
| Venice — Merchant Republic Archive District | VEN | The Venetian Republic in 1367; the archive district near the Rialto; the Canal between counting-houses; the smell of canal water and Levantine spices; the Council of Ten's shadow over commercial dispute resolution; the most sophisticated commercial legal apparatus in the Mediterranean | Commercial law, the canceled commission, retroactive nullification disputes, the Venetian factor as institutional proxy |
| Aleppo — Storytellers' and Merchant Quarter | ALP | Aleppo in 1367, under Mamluk administration: the Syrian Christian and Muslim quarters of the city, the coffee-house and market district where hakawati storytellers perform daily, the private courts of scholar-physicians who record oral traditions; a city where the stories have never been written and the people who carry them move between market, women's quarter, and caravanserai without any administrative record noting their profession; the smell of cedar resin, spice trade, and old paper in the physician's back room | Oral tradition, first inscription, the collector who disclaims authorship, the storyteller community as legal entity, the manuscript market at the edge of a living performance tradition |
| Latakia — Syrian Mediterranean Port | LAT | The port city on the Syrian coast, 1367, under Mamluk administration: the harbor where Venetian and Genoese factors load manuscripts and materials for European collections; the quayside where a professional manuscript dealer can intercept a carrier and make an offer in full view of the harbormaster; the last point where a manuscript in transit can still be redirected; the smell of salt and cedar and ink and the efficiency of a professional who has done this transaction many times before | The final interception point, commercial manuscript trade, prior-acquisition protocols, the last Mediterranean port before the northern overland route |
| Cairo — Booksellers' Quarter and Sufi Hospice District | CAI | Cairo in 1367, Mamluk period: the manuscript market near al-Azhar mosque, cedar-box manuscripts in stacked shelves, the bookseller who has three competing buyers in the parlor and a court official's men on the street; the Sufi hospice district nearby, where scholars and mystics maintain private libraries under the Sultan's supervision; the smell of old paper, river-water from the Nile, incense from the mosques, and the specific tension of a city where the Caliph signs documents and the Sultan runs everything | Manuscript custody, private speech under institutional constraint, the neutral carrier as fourth option, the Mamluk chancery as document-control system |
| Baghdad — Abbasid Legacy, 1367 Scholar and Sufi Quarter | BGD | Baghdad under Jalayirid rule, 1367: the city of the House of Wisdom, still carrying that identity even after the Mongol destruction of 1258; a Sufi intellectual center alongside the surviving scholarly institutions; the smell of river-water and incense and the cooking-fires of a dense city; the Tigris visible from the scholar quarter; the specific texture of a city that builds its institutions on the memory of what was destroyed | Consciousness studies, Sufi treatises, medical-theological hybrid scholarship, the archive that receives claims that do not fit standard categories |

---

| Bistritz — Saxon Market Town | BIS | Eastern Transylvanian market town, 1367 under Hungarian crown / Saxon self-governance; a town that has been burned five times and rebuilt each time; the smell of pine smoke and paprika and old stone; the road east leads to the Borgo Pass and then to Bukovina; the road west leads toward Klausenburg; every known superstition in the world gathered into the horseshoe of the Carpathians, of which this town is one edge | The threshold of the known world, the place where western travelers begin to notice they have entered something older, the town that warns |
| Klausenburg — Principal City of Transylvania | KLZ | The largest city of Transylvania under Hungarian crown authority, 1367; Saxon, Magyar, and Vlach populations under one civic administration; a cathedral precinct, an archdeacon's court, a royal registry; the smell of wax candles, stone, and the specific dust of administrative buildings that are also old churches | Institutional authority at the center of a contested territory, the place where the official record is made, the archdeacon's desk as the hinge between witness and permanent record |
| Sibiu — Hermannstadt, Saxon Fortified City | SIB | The Saxon capital of southern Transylvania, 1367; heavily fortified; the Scholomance legend associated with a lake near here; a wealthy town whose wealth comes from being on the road south to Wallachia; the smell of German baking and Wallachian leather trade and the specific cold of fortified stone in autumn | The town at the edge of the supernatural, the road to Wallachia, the legend of the school of dark knowledge, the wealthy place that knows something lives in the hills it cannot name |
| Borgo Pass — Mountain Road Junction | BOR | The high pass road between Bistritz and Bukovina: grey rock, pine, wolf-sound at night, blue flames in the snow that appear and disappear; the road that has been kept intentionally bad since the time of the Hospadars, who did not want to make it easy for foreign troops; the specific feeling of a place designed to delay | The threshold between known and unknown, the road that was kept difficult on purpose, the place where natural and unnatural both walk the same ground |
| Buda — Capital of the Hungarian Kingdom | BDA | Buda in 1367, under Louis I of Hungary; the western bank of the Danube; the royal court, the royal registry, the Hungarian administrative apparatus at the center of the kingdom that controls Transylvania; the smell of limestone, river water, and court document ink | Royal authority, the center of the legal system that governs Transylvania from a distance, the place where Transylvanian deeds are enrolled and made permanent |

| Florence — Guild and Merchant City | FLR | Florence in 1367, 19 years after the Black Death; the wool guild's counting houses on the Arno, the ecclesiastical court district, the notarial offices near the Baptistery; the smell of wool-dye and candle tallow and old parchment; a city rebuilt by people who remember the empty houses; the specific confidence of a mercantile republic that handles grief by returning to work | Commercial attestation, guild-grade documentation, the notarial machine that certifies everything including fraud, the city that produced Boccaccio and knows it |
| Humanist Scholar's Villa | HMN | A hillside villa three hours north of Florence: terracotta roof, a lemon tree in the courtyard, old paper smell in every corridor, Ser Goffredo da Prato in a chair by the window that looks down the valley toward the city; a man who has been reading for sixty years and whose hands shake but whose eyes do not; the villa of a man who knows his work is almost done and is using the remaining time to be certain rather than swift | Dying witness testimony, the authentication that can only come from personal knowledge of the hand, the beloved teacher's legacy arriving at the last possible moment before the one person who can confirm it is gone |
| Naples — Southern Italian Port City and Ecclesiastical Court | NAP | Naples in 1367, under Angevin rule; the ecclesiastical court district near the harbor, the salt smell and the sound of sea birds and the particular quality of light on Angevin stone; a city that is both a major court destination and a major transit point; the harbor visible from the court's upper windows | Ecclesiastical filing, document classification, the court that decides what category a thing belongs to before it enters the permanent record, Andreuccio's city of three disasters |
| Genoa — Merchant Republic, Ligurian Harbor | GEN | Genoa in 1367, at the height of Genoese commercial reach from the Black Sea to the Atlantic; the harbor district, the consulate offices, the notarial quarter above the port; the smell of salt and ship-tar and the cedar of packed document chests; a city that administers empire by paper and by armed ships, in equal measure | Commercial law, notarial authority, document manufacture at scale, the city that controls Caffa and Chios and Famagusta from counting-house desks a thousand miles away |
| Bologna — University City and Ecclesiastical Road Junction | BOL | Bologna in 1367, on the Via Emilia between Florence and the north; the oldest university in Europe fifty years old and still accumulating; the ecclesiastical archive district; the Dominican and Franciscan houses that serve as document-custody institutions; the smell of academic parchment and clay-tile dust and northern Italian autumn | Scholarly review, the institutional reader who evaluates document contents, the road junction between Mediterranean origins and northern archives, the place where Fra Cipolla's coals and angel-feathers would both be studied if they arrived |
| Alexandria — Mamluk-Administered Egyptian Port | ALE | Alexandria in 1367, under Mamluk rule; the harbor with Genoese and Venetian and Catalan factors' houses on the quayside; the remains of the great library's legacy dispersed into private collections and mosque schools; the smell of salt and cumin and Levantine commerce; Saladin's city in the Decameron's imagination, where Messer Torello slept one night and woke in Pavia | The impossible journey's origin point, the document that travels faster than any rider, the generosity of the distant host, East meeting West at its most commercial and its most chivalric simultaneously |

| Bordeaux — English Gascon Capital | BDX | Bordeaux in 1367, capital of the Black Prince's Principality of Aquitaine: wine-merchants' quarter on the Garonne, a requisitioned inn district where the Free Companies lodge between engagements, the smell of river-water and wine-oak and oiled iron; the Black Prince's court two streets up from the harbor wall; the company forming in the road outside; a city organizing the world's departure to Spain and not looking northeast at Périgord | Routier economy, safe-conduct commerce, the neutral carrier, the city where war and paperwork share the same inn |
| Avignon — Papal Palace Administrative District | AVG | Avignon in 1367, seat of the papal court in the Comtat Venaissin: the Palais des Papes above the city, the apostolic chamber in the administrative wing, the papal gate where secretaries and cardinals' representatives wait with offers; the smell of incense and legal dust and the specific atmosphere of a city that is simultaneously the church's administrative capital and a prisoner of its own prestige; the Rhône bridge visible from the upper window; Avignon was the pope's city but the companies were twelve leagues away and everyone knew it | Institutional grace under duress, the category heading chosen for survival, the sealed extract as the document of record, the cardinal's men at the gate as the last obstacle before the road south, the price of peace recorded as charity |
| Périgord Forest Road | PER | The limestone-ridge forest road between the Garonne valley and the Dordogne, 1367: narrow defile between pale rock faces, the road surfaced by use rather than maintenance, wolf-tracks in the mud at dawn; outriders visible at distance before they become problems; the ground no administration claims and every armed man uses; where the routier economy operates most openly because no one with authority is watching | Liminal jurisdiction, the carrier exposed, the gap between institutional control, the place where the agreement is tested |
| Beaulieu-en-Périgord — Limestone Valley Town | SRL | A Périgord valley town in 1367: golden limestone walls at noon and grey at dusk, a main gate with a wicket grate, the town hall on the market square, a council chamber with a long table and tallow candles; a town that has paid for protection and is deciding whether the receipt held; the smell of limestone dust and autumn smoke and the specific fear of people who correctly identified that the letter in transit is all that stands between them and a bad season | Received obligation, the town council as court of final receipt, the agreement's last mile, the question of what was actually paid |

| Ragusa (Dubrovnik) — Dalmatian Republic, Adriatic Road Junction | RGS | Ragusa in 1367, the small merchant republic on the Dalmatian coast that stays neutral between Venice and Hungary by careful diplomatic management; the waystation inns on the road north from the city to the overland route; the smell of Adriatic salt and road-mud and the specific quality of an inn where nobody is from the same place; a road junction where Mediterranean document traffic meets the northern overland network | Diplomatic neutrality, the professional's last offer, the carrier between worlds, the waystation as the last moment a thing can be redirected |

| Sibyl's Vault Archive | SVA | Deep room cut into the tufa rock behind the oracle grotto at Cumae, southern Italy: six niches in the stone wall, the oracle seat worn smooth at the entrance, cool air and old wax smell, the iron case in the third niche at the back; Carmela di Cumae, sixth keeper in an unbroken informal line; the Sibyl's prophetic records that no living scholar knows exist, held by inheritance rather than appointment | Prophetic authority, the divine naming as geographic record, the keeper who inherited rather than was appointed, the document that predates any administrative claim |
| Cape Palinurus Harbor Town | PAC | A small fishing port at the base of Cape Palinurus, southern Italy: thirty fishing boats, one tavern, a harbor master's office with new ducal charts posted — Promontorio del Duca — and an old pilots' guild log that has always said Capo Palinuro; guild master Piero, fifty years old, forty years on this coast; the harbor pilots who have been fined this week for using the old name | Geographic continuity, the pilots' guild as second authority, the name that has never stopped being used, the harbor navigated longer than the duke's family has held the province |
| Naples Maritime Registry | NMR | The administrative chart office for southern Italian coastal geography, Naples: stone building above the harbor district, three clerks, long folio tables, Ser Matteo di Napoli as registry master; receives both commercial and prophetic-religious geographic records when the category can be established; the geographic records folio with blank lines at the bottom waiting for new categories | Chart authority, the registry that establishes what a place is named before adjudicating what it should be called, the Divine Naming Category as a new record type, the maritime register that can override a duke's charts |
| Court Physician's Study | PHY | A small room off the main corridor of the great hall: herb-smell and tallow, a single window, a table with clay vessels and linen bandages; physician Maro with twenty years of experience and a cedar tablet she cannot fill herself; the cell door visible at the end of the corridor through the study window | The limit of medical authority, the instrument that requires the warrior's own act to become useful, the keeper who has run out of approaches |
| Guard House Cell | GHC | A stone cell in the guard house annex: low ceiling, flat stone floor, a single iron lamp on a hook; the warrior in a panther's skin against the far wall; the smell of stone and old straw and the wilderness that came in with him; the cell where grief has become an identity and where four conversations over one afternoon might change a man's mind about whether to remain | Grief as spiritual discipline, the question that breaks the silence, the difference between hope and obligation, the cell as the place where the inside and outside of a person are finally the same |
| Bosphorus Thracian Noble Estate | BTR | A Thracian noble family's estate near the Bosphorus, 1367: a stone archive room off the main hall, cedar chests of territorial records, the smell of old parchment and family wax; steward Dragomir at the table with the grant record between them; the harbor visible through the high window; a family that has been deciding for four months whether its history is a liability or an obligation | Territorial record custody, the neutral carrier vs. the agent distinction, the family archive as a place where history becomes either patrimony or liability, the six-generation chain of inheritance |
| Naxos Ducal Monastery Library | NXS | The library of the main monastery on Naxos under Venetian ducal authority, 1367: stone shelves, the smell of old binding and Aegean salt air, a reading stand by the window where the morning light is best; Brother Grigorios's desk in the corner of the cosmography collection; the harbor and the Venetian factor's office visible below through the high window; the place where a Plutarch preface sat for four years in a theological collection where it was not being looked for | Unexpected find in wrong collection, the scholar who holds back from uncertainty rather than fear, the document that needs a carrier who understands its category, Venetian administrative authority over island scholarly patrimony |

| Baghdad — Sufi Scholar Quarter | BAG | Jalayirid Baghdad, 1367, under Sultan Uvays I: the post-Abbasid scholarly quarter near the great mosque complex; Sufi lodges and philosophy schools in the alleys behind the market; the smell of old paper and incense; scholars who survived the Mongol disruption by inheriting the libraries of those who did not; the place where Islamic philosophy and Sufi thought converge and where private letters to scholars in Basra are sometimes intercepted at the south gate | Post-Mongol manuscript culture, Sufi intellectual networks, the correspondence between Baghdad and Basra as a route that judicial authorities can monitor, the letter as risk |
| Basra — River City | BAS | Basra in 1367, at the Tigris-Euphrates delta: a trading city under Jalayirid administration, with a small but active circle of independent scholars including women scholars of legal theory; river-boat traffic from Baghdad; the harbor district, the judicial court in the civic quarter, the private households in the scholar's row where the court's administrative reach is less immediate; the smell of river mud and tallow and the specific tension of a city where a judicial review is pending | Independent scholarship under institutional pressure, the judicial court as antagonist, the private household as the last space where a document can be received without inspection, the river road back to Baghdad as the route the agents take |
| Pistoia — Retired Judge's Quarter | PIS | A Tuscan commune town under Florentine orbit, 1367; the Via dei Notai with its retired legal professionals; cedar-smell and old paper; a man who wrote a ruling thirty years ago still has it in his personal files and can be found at his desk any morning; the road south to Prato visible from the upper story window | Personal legal custody, retired judicial authority, the document that already exists and simply needs to be retrieved and certified |
| Prato — Commune Court and Guild District | PRA | A Tuscan commune town north of Florence, 1367, recently under Florentine protection; the podestà's court adjacent to the main square; the guild counting houses in the lane behind; the smell of wool and old stone and the specific quality of a city that handles its own affairs within limits it has agreed to accept; Datini's city twenty years before Datini becomes famous | The communal court as the arena of the already-decided, the statute that protects waiting in a retired judge's papers to arrive in time, the morning session as the irreversible threshold |
| Tabriz — Jalayirid Scholar Quarter | TBZ | Tabriz in 1367 under Jalayirid Sultan Uvays I: the northern Iranian silk-road city rebuilt from Mongol-period disruption, now a major administrative and scholarly center in Azerbaijan; the scholar quarter adjacent to the main bazaar, with manuscript dealers, copyists' workshops, the smell of ink and mountain air; Ahmad al-Farghani's consulting room near the central mosque; a city where Persian, Turkic, and Arabic scholarship converge and where Ilkhanid-period archival networks still operate at reduced capacity | Post-Mongol scholarly recovery, the manuscript dealer as intermediary between discovery and evaluation, Persian epic scholarship in an administratively Turkic city, the authentication network as a web of personal trust maintained across generations |
| Trebizond — Byzantine Scholarly Port | TRB | The Komnenian Greek city on the Black Sea coast, 1367: the last Byzantine successor state at the eastern end of the Pontic shore; Greek paleographic tradition maintained in monastery libraries above the harbor; the port district where Caspian-route merchants meet Genoese factors; Nikephoros Katakalon's study in the monastic archive, with its view of the harbor and its wall of Armenian and Greek attestation records; a city that has survived by being between everyone and beholden to none | Byzantine Greek paleographic tradition, Pontic Greek scholarship as a bridging archive between east and west, the monastery as the container for a secular certification practice, attestation as the service a small surviving empire offers in exchange for remaining viable |
| Yazd — Zoroastrian Fire Temple Quarter | YAZ | The desert city of Yazd in central Persia, 1367: a city with a continuous Zoroastrian community maintaining fire temples while under Muzaffarid Muslim administration; the fire temple quarter with its distinctive wind-tower architecture and the perpetual flame tended by the Dastur's household; the smell of cedar and dry desert stone; a community that keeps its records in a language the city administration cannot read and whose theological archives contain historical documents maintained by treating them as devotional objects | Continuous community memory, Zoroastrian documentary preservation as devotional practice, the fire temple as a non-Islamic archive, the document that survived because its custodians treated it as sacred rather than useful |
| Kabul — Mountain Fortress District | KBL | Kabul in 1367, in the Hindu Kush, under contested local authority at the edge of Muzaffarid and Sultanate of Delhi spheres; the fortress district above the main bazaar with its view of the mountain passes; caravan-route merchants, fortress administrators, a small scholarly community maintaining manuscripts in a city that is more crossroads than destination; the cold mountain air and the specific quality of light at altitude that makes reading a daylight activity | Silk Road crossroads scholarship, the fortress as archive container, manuscripts maintained at altitude by a community that knows it sits between multiple empires, the caravan route as the delivery path for both documents and danger |
| Sístán — Province Road Town | SIS | Eastern Persia at the Afghan border, 1367: the arid semi-desert province of Sístán under shifting local administration; a road town on the Silk Road branch crossing from the province toward Nishapur and Herat; the desert caravanserai with its central courtyard well, sparse shade, and the smell of dust and old paper in the saddle-bag; a community that maintains its cultural memory through oral tradition and portable manuscripts because the architecture of the province has not encouraged permanence | Portable document culture, the caravan archive, the road town as the place where Sístán's Rostam traditions enter the Silk Road network, oral-manuscript interface in a region where the legend is native |
| Herat — Library and Scholar Quarter | HRT | Herat in 1367 under the Kart dynasty: a major eastern Persian city with a significant manuscript library and a scholar's quarter that is already one of the great intellectual centers of the Persian world; the city that will become the Timurid capital in thirty years; under pressure from Timur's expanding campaigns even in 1367; the library complex with its reading rooms and cataloguing alcoves, the smell of high-altitude air and cedar boards and old binding; scholars who know their city may change masters and are managing their collections with that knowledge | Major library collection, end-of-dynasty document management, the scholar who is building for successors he cannot name, the city that will become a future capital — the library is already ready, waiting for the administration to catch up |
| Erzincan — Eastern Anatolian Fortress-Town | ERZ | Erzincan in 1367, in the eastern Anatolian highlands under the Eretna principality: a fortress-town above the Euphrates bend at the junction of the Anatolian plateau and the Armenian highland corridor; garrison district with weapons workshops and an Armenian scholarly community; the smell of cold mountain stone and metalwork; the road west to Trabzon via the Zigana pass begins here; a city that lives by knowing which direction the next army will come from and keeping records accordingly | Weapons scholarship, Armenian-Persian cultural interface, fortress archive as repository of technical knowledge, the mountain road to Trebizond as the route between the frontier and the Black Sea |
| Mosul — Tigris City, Jalayirid–Mamluk Border Zone | MOS | Mosul in 1367 under Jalayirid administration: a major Tigris city at the contested border between Jalayirid and Mamluk spheres; the qadi's court and the scholars' quarter in the old city; the Nineveh plain visible across the river; a city where legal authority is more stable than political authority, so the courts function even when the border shifts; the smell of river-water, limestone dust, and old paper; legal scholars who understand their texts will be used by parties on both sides of every dispute | Legal scholarship under contested sovereignty, the qadi's court as neutral ground, succession cases involving pre-Islamic precedent, the Tigris as both boundary and corridor |
| Merv — Khorasan Oasis Scholarly City | MRV | Merv in 1367, in eastern Khorasan at the edge of the Muzaffarid sphere and the emerging Timurid pressure zone: one of the great oasis cities of the Silk Road, a major center of Islamic jurisprudence and theological scholarship; the madrasa quarter around the Seljuk-era mosque complex; the smell of desert air and old binding; a city that has survived multiple conquest cycles by being too useful to destroy and too learned to ignore; scholars who write opinions that will be used by courts on three sides of every political dispute | Khorasan legal scholarship, the jurist who must anticipate how the ruling will be used, Shafi'i and Hanafi jurisprudence in dialogue, the oasis city as the stable intellectual anchor in an unstable political region |
| Bursa — Ottoman Anatolian City, Bosphorus Approach | BUR | Bursa in 1367, first Ottoman capital before Constantinople: a prosperous Anatolian city under firm Ottoman administration, thirty kilometers from the Bosphorus ferry crossing; the old Byzantine church-quarter alongside the new Ottoman mosque-madrasa complex; the smell of silk, bath-house steam, and cedar; a city at the junction between Ottoman Anatolia and the Greek-speaking Byzantine world across the water; scholars who must calibrate their work to administrative categories that the next city does not share | Ottoman-Byzantine legal interface, the checkpoint as the threshold between administrative regimes, women's scholarly work under two different institutional frameworks, the ferry crossing as the moment of category change |
| Caffa — Genoese Black Sea Colony | CAF | Caffa (Kaffa) in 1367 on the Crimean peninsula, the Genoese colonial port on the Black Sea: the most important Genoese commercial and administrative center in the eastern Mediterranean after Constantinople; the harbor with Genoese factors' counting-houses, a significant Armenian and Greek scholarly community alongside the Italian merchants, the smell of salt and tallow and wool; the observatory on the cliff above the harbor where Armenian and Greek astronomers maintain navigational records for the Black Sea trade routes; the city where three scholarly traditions work together under Italian commercial authority | Genoese colonial administration, Armenian-Greek-Italian scholarly interface, Black Sea navigational astronomy, the commission that serves commercial interests while doing scholarly work |
| Tana — Golden Horde Trading Post, Don Delta | TAN | Tana at the Don river delta, 1367: a mixed Venetian-Genoese-Golden Horde trading post at the edge of the Eurasian steppe; the main overland route from the Black Sea into the Golden Horde territory starts here; artisans' workshops alongside the mercantile factor houses; mapmakers and instrument-makers serving both the Black Sea trade and the steppe routes; the smell of river mud, leather, and trade goods from three directions; a city whose authority is always contested but whose function is never in doubt | Steppe-maritime interface, contested colonial authority, the commercial commission as the cover for scholarly work, the mapmaker as the node where navigational data becomes physical artifact |
| Bukhara — Timurid Central Asia, Scholar Quarter | BUK | Bukhara in 1367, under the emerging Timurid sphere of influence: one of the great Islamic scholarly cities, the Mir-i Arab madrasa complex as its intellectual center; the bazaar quarter with its manuscript dealers and the calligraphers' row; the smell of old binding, saffron, and the specific quality of Zerafshan valley air; a city that has changed hands between the Chagatai, the Ilkhanids, and now Timur's forces without its scholarly identity shifting; the place where thirty-year scholarly quests for specific manuscripts have a realistic chance of conclusion | Central Asian manuscript culture, lineage scholarship, the madrasa as the institution that survives political change, the manuscript dealer as the node between private collection and scholarly access |
| Saray — Golden Horde Winter Court, Volga Delta | SAR | Saray (New Saray, Saray al-Jadid) in 1367 at the Volga delta: capital of the Golden Horde under Khan Aziz; the Khan's winter court complex with its administrative buildings, diplomatic residences, and the hall of morning audience; the smell of the steppe river, felt yurt lining, and the specific quality of a court where Central Asian, Mongol, and Islamic administrative traditions sit in the same building; a city of political faction and careful diplomatic communication | Golden Horde diplomatic context, the factional court as obstacle, the diplomatic delegation as the recipient of correspondence that factions want to delay, the steppe route as the delivery path that bypasses official channels |
| Beirut — Mamluk Syrian Coast City | BYR | Beirut in 1367 under Mamluk administration: a harbor city on the Syrian coast between Sidon and Tripoli; smaller than Aleppo or Damascus but a significant transit point for Venetian and Genoese eastern trade; the shrine-record-keeper's quarter in the Christian neighborhood, the harbour-side mosque and the Mamluk factor's house; a city where the specific vocation of cross-tradition document preservation has a community of practitioners; the smell of sea salt and fig-wood smoke | Cross-tradition document specialists, the Mamluk coast road as the delivery corridor from the north, the shrine record as the form that preserves vow documents across traditions, the harbor as the point where eastern and western Mediterranean trade routes exchange cargo and documents |
| Amasya — Pontic Anatolian Highland City | AMY | Amasya in 1367, in the Yeşilırmak valley of the Pontic highlands: a city of scholars and administrators under the Eretna principality, transitioning toward Ottoman influence; the old Pontic Greek city transformed into an Islamic scholarly center; the citadel above the valley, the medrese below; the smell of the river and walnut trees and the specific quality of a highland city that produces both jurists and generals; the road west to Sivas follows the Yeşilırmak valley | Pontic highland scholarship, the transition between Greek-Byzantine and Islamic scholarly tradition at the same river valley, the road to central Anatolia as the scholarly corridor, the city that produces legal arguments that travel |
| Sivas — Central Anatolian Scholarly City | SIV | Sivas in 1367, in central Anatolia under the Eretna principality: a major administrative and scholarly center on the high Anatolian plateau; the medrese and the caravanserai in the same district; the smell of mountain air, dust, and old stone; a city known for legal and philosophical scholarship with practical orientation — scholars who write arguments that will be used in qadi courts the same week; the road east to Amasya and west toward Ottoman territory | Central Anatolian Islamic jurisprudence, the practical seminar as the scholarly form, legal arguments flowing between scholarly colleagues and qadi courts, the city at the edge of multiple principalities |
| Thessaloniki — Byzantine-Controlled Port City | THE | Thessaloniki in 1367 under Byzantine administration: the second city of the Byzantine Empire, a major port on the Aegean, with a significant Greek scholarly community; the Byzantine court patronized scholarship in history, military affairs, and philosophy; the university district adjacent to the harbor; the smell of sea air and old stone and the particular quality of a city that knows it is the most important Byzantine city after the capital itself; the Via Egnatia arrives here from the west | Byzantine military history scholarship, the court-patronized commission as the scholarly framework, the Via Egnatia as the delivery corridor from Constantinople, the port as the final Byzantine waypoint before the western routes |
| Monastery of the Latomos — Upper Thessaloniki | LMO | Upper city monastery in Thessaloniki, 1367: library window overlooking the harbor, manuscript collection being quietly dispersed west ahead of Ottoman pressure, a Serbian administrator conducting property inventories; the place where a scholar chose the most difficult document last because it was the hardest to justify moving | Ottoman pressure on Greek monastic collections, the quiet manuscript dispersal as institutional survival, the administrator's inventory as the obstacle, the harbor visible below as the departure point |
| Thessaloniki Harbor — Active Port District | THA | The active harbor of Thessaloniki, 1367: Venetian and Genoese merchants, westbound Adriatic crossings, a morning ship departing before the harbor master's paperwork catches up; the last Greek city on the westbound route before the Adriatic crossing; the quayside factor's offices and the galley schedules; the smell of salt and wood-smoke and the specific urgency of a port that has more departures than arrivals in 1367 | The westward departure point from Greek territory, the ship that leaves before the document's legal complications catch up, the harbor master's manifest as the last official record, the Adriatic as the threshold between Byzantine and Italian jurisdictions |
| Nicaea — Ottoman-Administered Former Byzantine Capital | NIC | Nicaea (Iznik) in 1367 under Ottoman administration since 1331: the former seat of the Byzantine Empire in exile (1204–1261), now an Ottoman administrative city retaining a Greek-language scholarly community; the old Byzantine church and medrese in the same neighborhood; the Bithynian plain surrounding the city; the smell of fig trees and old stone and the quality of a city that remembers being the capital of something larger; scholars navigating Ottoman academic registration requirements | Former Byzantine intellectual center under Ottoman administration, the Greek scholarly community persisting under new authority, the Ottoman academic registry as the bureaucratic obstacle, proximity to Bursa as the Ottoman capital connection |
| Amid — Jalayirid Jazira Fortress City | AMD | Amid (Diyarbakır) in 1367 under Jalayirid Sultanate control: the black basalt city on the upper Tigris bend, its walls cut from the same volcanic stone as the plateau; the great mosque and the qadi's court in the same neighborhood as the Tigris-view terrace where merchants catalog goods crossing between Jazira and Anatolia; a frontier city between Jalayirid and Mamluk administrative zones, its theological community accustomed to working under two legal systems simultaneously; the smell of the Tigris, black stone in summer sun, and the specific quality of a city whose walls are made of the same material as the ground they stand on | Jalayirid-Mamluk frontier zone, the Tigris as the city's organizational axis, the theological community operating across two legal jurisdictions, the Jazira road as the delivery corridor connecting upper Tigris cities to the Syrian network |
| Tunis — Hafsid Sultanate Capital | TUN | Tunis in 1367 under Hafsid Sultanate rule: the major North African port city on the Gulf of Tunis, capital of the western Mediterranean's most significant Islamic scholarly center outside Egypt; al-Zaytuna mosque and madrasa as the intellectual center; the medina's souks organized by trade and scholarship simultaneously; the harbor connecting Tunisian scholars to Sicilian, Venetian, and Genoese trade routes; a city where North African Maliki legal tradition meets Mediterranean commercial law in the same qadi court; Ibn Khaldun had been educated and politically active in this city and its vicinity for thirty years by 1367 | Hafsid Maliki legal tradition, the Mediterranean commercial port as the delivery terminus, al-Zaytuna as the scholarly authority node, the North African caravan network connecting Tunis to the trans-Saharan trade, the harbor as the point where African and European legal systems exchange documents |
| Fez — Marinid Sultanate, Western Mediterranean | FEZ | Fez in 1367 under Marinid Sultanate rule: the double city — old Fez (Fes al-Bali) with al-Qarawiyyin mosque-university, one of the oldest continuously operating universities in the world, and new Fez (Fes al-Jdid) with the royal palace and Marinid administrative buildings; the tanneries, the dye pits, the medersas, the smell of cedar and leather and the specific quality of a city that has been producing Islamic scholarship continuously for five hundred years; the Marinids were weakening in 1367 but the scholarly tradition was not; a western terminus of Saharan and Mediterranean trade routes | Maliki legal scholarship at al-Qarawiyyin, the Marinid administrative court as the obstacle or patron depending on document type, the western Mediterranean as the delivery context, the trans-Saharan connection as the source of categorical legal questions the eastern tradition has not yet addressed |
| Ankara — Ottoman-Controlled Anatolian City | ANK | Ankara in 1367 under Ottoman administration: the old Galatian and Byzantine city on the central Anatolian plateau, now an Ottoman administrative center controlling the roads between Anatolia's Black Sea ports and the interior; the central mosque and medrese in the old city; the Ottoman bey's garrison at Kastamonu controls the northern approach; a city at the intersection of Black Sea trade routes and Persian highland roads; scholars navigating Ottoman academic registration requirements while drawing on the Byzantine and Islamic traditions simultaneously | Ottoman Anatolian administrative center, the medrese as the scholarly venue, cross-traditional philosophical exchange between Pontic Greek and Islamic scholars, the Kastamonu checkpoint as the northern gate to the Ottoman interior |
| Hamadan — Persian City, Jalayirid–Muzaffarid Zone | HAM | Hamadan in 1367 in the contested zone between the Jalayirid Sultanate to the north and the Muzaffarid Sultanate to the south: the ancient Persian city known for Ibn Sina's tomb and centuries of medical and philosophical scholarship; the medrese and the caravanserai in the old Median city center; the smell of mountain air and the dust of the high Persian plateau; scholars who combine philosophical rigor with practical medical application; a city accustomed to operating under multiple overlapping sovereignties and producing arguments that are accepted by both | Persian medical and philosophical scholarship, the old Avicennan tradition as the intellectual context, the bazaar manuscript market as the economic pressure on scholarly documents, the Jalayirid–Muzaffarid administrative overlap as the political context |
| Tbilisi — Georgian Scholarly City | TIF | Tbilisi in 1367 under the Kingdom of Georgia, in the period of recovery from Mongol incursions: the Caucasian city on the Kura River, center of Georgian intellectual and theological life; the Georgian Orthodox church and the scholarly community in the same neighborhood as the Persian-inflected bazaar district; a city where Byzantine Neoplatonic philosophy, Georgian theological tradition, and Persian literary culture meet in the same scholars' reading rooms; the mountain approach from the west is the Armenian connection; the mountain passes to the east connect to the Persian and Caucasian trade networks | Georgian Neoplatonic philosophy, the Byzantine-inflected scholarly tradition in a Caucasian context, cross-cultural philosophical exchange between Armenian and Georgian traditions, the Kura River valley as the delivery route between the Armenian highlands and the eastern Caucasus |
| Adrianople — Ottoman Capital, European Thrace | ADR | Adrianople (Edirne) in 1367, Ottoman capital since Sultan Murad I relocated the court there: the former Byzantine fortress city in Thrace on the Meriç River, now the administrative center of the European Ottoman provinces; the old Byzantine churches alongside new Ottoman administrative buildings; the scholars navigating between Byzantine intellectual tradition and Ottoman court requirements; the Via Egnatia checkpoint at the city's western approach; a city that is simultaneously a frontier post and a capital, aware of its own transitional status | Ottoman European administration, the court as the source of scholarly patronage and also the primary regulatory authority, Byzantine scholars operating under Ottoman administrative tolerance, the Via Egnatia as the delivery corridor from Thessaloniki, the Thracian road network as the connection to Bulgaria and the Balkans |

| FCO-02 | The Painter's Afterword | 3 | CRT → NAP → WM | Paid craftsman accounts for depicting grief |
| FCO-04 | The Buthrotum Register | 4 | VEN → RGS → WM | Grief organized into governance; the replica city's founding record |
| FCO-05 | Barce's Account | 5 | GEN → BOL → WM | Survivor's testimony contradicts the monument in three places |
| FCO-07 | The Sibyl's Sealed Jar | 6 | NAP → RME → WM | Fulfilled prophecy is more dangerous than false; archive receives it neutrally |
| FCO-09 | The Mensas Plate | 7 | RME → WM | The relic of comprehension — someone inscribed the moment of understanding, not the event |
| FCO-14 | The Fragment of the Final Line | 8 | MTC → RME → WM | The alternate ending that survived in a margin; three words that change the ethics of the close |
| FCO-03 | Creusa's Third Temple | 9 | NAP → RME → WM | The votary inscription for the woman who vanished; the dedication names her as still present; some losses are addressed rather than concluded |
| FCO-06 | Palinurus's Chart | 10 | NAP → VRN → WM | The helmsman's chart survived the helmsman; used again 1,300 years later; navigational knowledge outlasts its keeper |
| FCO-08 | Phlegyas's Inscription | 11 | VRN → BOL → WM | The inscription that reads differently from every doorway; the archive holds it without claiming to know which reading is correct |
| FCO-10 | The Shield's Cartographer | 12 | VEN → RME → WM | The commentary that proves too much; the humanist's annotations are more dangerous than the text; the suppressed reading is the archive's primary value |
| FCO-11 | Euryalus's Mother's Tablet | 13 | NAP → WM | The message that was never sent; the unfinished letter is the most complete record; the archive receives what was interrupted |
| FCO-12 | The Sword Belt of Pallas | 14 | FLR → RME → WM | The object that became evidence after the act; the fitting proves the cause was warranted; the archive receives it as material witness, not trophy |
| FCO-13 | Acca's Message | 15 | NAP → BOL → WM | The record of the conveyer, not the message; named in stone for what she carried; the archive invents a category to receive her name in the role |
| NWI-06 | The Cretan's Account | 3 | SIN → CON → WM | Front-rank view of communal salvation; the shout coming from the front and keeping coming |
| NWI-03 | Proxenus's Letter | 4 | ORC → ATH → RGS → WM | The invitation is a form of authorship; Proxenus made the Anabasis possible |
| NWI-05 | The Salmydessus Books | 5 | VAR → CON → WM | Evidence of loss is a primary document; the catalog proves the text existed |
| WAW-07 | The Mile-Marker Account | 3 | ROM → WM | Secular administrative witness; the road official did not know what he saw |
| WAW-04 | Chilo's Account | 4 | ATH → CON → RGS → WM | The informer's register becomes the martyrs' witness; dual accession, one chain of custody |
| WAW-05 | Acte's Letter | 5 | ROM → FLR → WM | The letter that will soon stop being true; filed under the act, not the man |
| MLA-04 | Tiro's Notes | 3 | NAP → BOL → WM | The shorthand draft is primary testimony; what the orator thought before he decided what to say |
| MLA-02 | Alcibiades's Letter | 4 | ALE → RGS → VEN → WM | Treasonous and honest simultaneously; filed under stateless actor, not Athens |
| MLA-06 | The Agrarian Commission Record | 5 | ROM → BOL → WM | The historical document that is still a live lawsuit; archive suspends evidentiary function |
| BGW-04 | Morgiana's Grant | 3 | CAI → ALE → VEN → WM | The unanswerable question is the document's primary condition; archive creates category for structural undecidability |
| BGW-06 | The Translator's Preface | 4 | TBZ → CON → VEN → WM | The only part of the collection that does not pretend; translator's testimony filed as primary |
| BGW-07 | The Dunyazade Account | 5 | TUN → GEN → WM | The voice present for every story but absent from every story; first instance filed as recovered voice |
| CAI-06 | Ser Marco's Session Notes | 3 | ALP → ANT → WM | The oral tradition at the moment it becomes aware it is being written down; the session notes record the storyteller changing the story |
| CAI-03 | The Baghdad Mechanism Filing | 4 | BAG → TRB → ANT → WM | The archive receives claims, not verifications; guild patent for directed aerial transport filed under claimed technology |
| CAI-07 | The Aleppo Hills Survey | 5 | CON → WM | The neutral language of a geographic survey is itself the primary data; the surveyor describes what he saw without naming what it was |
| IST-04 | The Bryennios Draft | 3 | CON → RGS → WM | The unfinished draft survives because suppression requires publication; the husband's prior account names what the wife's defense required her to omit |
| IST-05 | The Devol Treaty Copy | 4 | PAR → RGS → WM | The document hidden by the losing party's own side — not to conceal defeat from the enemy but to conceal submission from allies |
| IST-06 | The Exile Letter | 5 | CON → WM | The commissioning letter for the archive's own contents; the origin of the collection is itself in the collection |
| IST-02 | The Curtain Notes | 6 | CON → THA → WM | The intelligence method preserved in its own product; the monks behind the curtain are inside the document they produced; heresy census and security record filed as one |
| IST-07 | The Physician's Notes | 7 | CON → ANC → WM | The clinical record beside the literary account; both are accurate; neither is the correction of the other; the archive receives the complete record |
| CAI-02 | Aladdin's Registration | 6 | DAM → ANT → WM | The blank form precedes the story; the story was constructed around a Syrian-Persian legal instrument type; the template and the narrative illuminate each other |
| CAI-04 | Prince Ahmed's Arrow | 7 | ALP → LAT → WM | The parish record of the unexplained; the account arrives and discovers the archive already holds the other end of it |
| CAI-05 | The Deathbed Instruction | 8 | DAM → ALE → WM | The legal instrument that is the story's emotional center; the story is the breach case that the form was designed to prevent |
| WAW-02 | The Charge List | 6 | ROM → FLR → WM | The prosecution document is the only census; the arrest record names the community no one else recorded |
| WAW-06 | Peter's Deposition | 7 | ANT → CON → RGS → WM | The periphery holds the origin; the Syrian community kept the commission in its original linguistic register before Greek transmission began |
| WAW-03 | The Arena Register | 8 | NAP → BOL → WM | The bureaucratic record of the event that witnesses understood as miraculous; the praetor filed it under crowd disorder; the administrative language is the only non-partisan account |
| MLA-03 | The Solon Tablet | 6 | MYS → ANC → WM | The authenticated copy that precedes the original's uncertain fate; the rubbing arrives before the stone; the copy is the only form in which the laws will reach the archive |
| MLA-05 | The Nicias Correspondence | 7 | THA → CON → ANC → WM | The source document behind the canonical account; the historian's selection is visible as selection; the archive holds what Thucydides chose not to quote |
| BGW-02 | The Sinbad Chart | 6 | FAM → VEN → WM | The navigational chart that matches the story's geography; the chart and the story share place names; the archive receives the cartographic evidence of Sinbad's world |
| BGW-03 | The Caliph's Night Log | 7 | BAG → TBZ → CON → WM | The private security log of the legendary ruler's nocturnal absences; the court chronicle recorded his governance; only the log recorded his nights |
| BGW-05 | The Genie Contract | 8 | CAI → FAM → WM | The legal document in which the impossible is an administrative category; the appraiser wrote 'not established' and moved on |
| NWI-02 | The Rearguard's Log | 6 | TBZ → CON → WM | The operational record versus the literary account; the day-log records what happened; the Anabasis records what it meant; both are primary |
| NWI-04 | The Oath at Calpe | 7 | VEN → WM | The diplomatic formula for sacred oaths; the tablet records the form Tissaphernes used; the formula makes the betrayal visible as deliberate violation |
| NWI-07 | The Scillus Deed | 8 | MYS → ANC → WM | The settlement inscription that closed the march; the archive holds start, march, and end; the complete arc is now in one collection |
| NWI-08 | The Towns That Were Not Given | 9 | CON → RGS → WM | The grant document for three towns the army earned and never received; the archive holds the unfulfilled promise alongside Xenophon's private farm |
| CAI-S09 | The Lamp Boy's Line | 9 | DAM → ALP → LAT → RGS → WM | The court ruling that accidentally created performance ownership |
| CAI-S10 | Diyab's Variant | 10 | ALP → LAT → CON → WM | The private letter as the only record; the dead addressee as preservation mechanism |
| CAI-S11 | Umm Sulayman's Six Variants | 11 | ALP → ANT → WM | The story as adjustable instrument; methodology not content; first performance variation record |
| CAI-S12 | The Hakawati License | 12 | DAM → LAT → RGS → WM | The routine license that accidentally implied administrative authority over oral tradition |
| CAI-S13 | The Performance Notation | 13 | ALP → ALX → ANT → WM | The description of what writing cannot contain; the archive holds the fact of the phrase |
| CAI-S14 | The Oral Genealogy | 14 | ALP → ALX → RGS → WM | Four honest diverging genealogies as a complete document; irresolution as the subject |
| BEY-S09 | The Intelligence Report | 8 | MRS → GEN → RGS → WM | The analysis too accurate to suppress; correct answer named wrong person's brother |
| BEY-S10 | The Chain Document | 9 | BEI → FAM → RGS → WM | The container's custody history as independent material corroboration; the cedar box is its own witness |
| BEY-S11 | The French-Speaking Lord | 10 | CAI → ALX → RGS → WM | The intelligence service in administrative language; filing without judgment; the family kept the record because destroying it required a verdict they hadn't reached |
| BEY-S12 | The Temple Entry Record | 11 | JER → SID → RGS → WM | Host's register and guest's memoir describe the same two hours; independence is stronger than collaboration; archive places them beside each other for the first time |
| BEY-S13 | The Liège Rubbing | 12 | LGE → COL → WM | The author who hid inside his book; the stone names the man the title page did not; the archive holds both without choosing |
| BEY-S14 | What the Pilgrim's Signet Looked Like | 13 | DAM → BEI → RGS → WM | The ordinary collection defines the extraordinary by contrast; the merchant's daughter saw what her father's curiosity was for |
| KYA-03 | The Fire Chain | 3 | YAZ → TBZ → WM | The fire cannot testify to its own origin; only the scroll with names from the first night constitutes evidence; the community that kept it is not the same as the property the court wants to seize |
| KYA-04 | The Demon's First Lesson | 4 | NIS → CON → WM | If authentic, the oldest writing lesson in the world; if not, the most accurate 9th-century imagining; the question is more valuable than either answer; the archive files both under the same category: Teaching Documents Under Duress |
| KYA-05 | The Princess of Zábulistán | 5 | KBL → TBZ → WM | The chronicle names the queen and records what she observed: "He stopped asking for counsel and began demanding confirmation"; Shah-Nameh Supplementary Records — Unnamed Women, Volume I |
| KYA-06 | The Record of Successive Honors | 6 | TBZ → RGS → WM | Six dynasties, same phrase — "added in acknowledgment of the banner's origin" — the guild ledger is the material history of the banner that outlasted every king who held it |
| KYA-07 | The Símúrgh's Second Feather | 7 | YAZ → CON → WM | The case has been sealed since the 11th century; whether the feather is inside is the question the archive holds; the sealed state is the primary archival condition |
| KYA-08 | The Caravanserai Account | 8 | SIS → SAM → WM | One hundred camels, salt, payment exact, men quiet, departed before first light, no trouble; civilian witness to a military event in the language of an innkeeper with no reason to notice |
| KYA-09 | The Trophy of the Broken Girdle | 9 | SIS → MRG → WM | The trophy is not of the kill; the trophy is of the grip; Rustem had Afrásiyáb once and the world did not end; the shrine was destroyed by Mongols; the rubbing is what remains |
| KYA-10 | The Second Bracelet | 10 | SIS → TBZ → WM | The clerk was performing his function — describing what the object did; what it did was remember; the bracelet kept in memory of the one given away is grief entered as inventory |
| KYA-11 | The Tree at the Border | 11 | SAM → CON → WM | The tree grew where the prince stepped; the botanist confirmed it exists and refused to confirm its origin; the archive files it under physical evidence because the tree exists regardless of what it grew from |
| KYA-12 | The Portrait Gallery | 12 | SAM → NIS → WM | The prince put both armies on his walls without comment and made an argument with paint; the palace was razed by the man who had him killed; the list is the only evidence the argument was made |
| KYA-13 | The King from the Sky | 13 | TBZ → CON → WM | The Chinese administrative record of the crash survives because the Chinese had no reason to manage the legend; the document that escaped the tradition's management is the outside view |
| KYA-14 | The Seeing Cup | 14 | MRG → TBZ → WM | The cup that could see the whole world was melted for coin; the sentence that said what the cup could do was not melted; the archive holds the capacity in the only form that survived the dispersal |
| KYA-15 | The Ring in the Bird | 15 | SAM → TBZ → WM | The ring appeared to Rustem twice and the killing happened anyway; the continuation tradition gave it a third appearance because a ring that stops killings had not yet stopped a killing |
| KYA-16 | The Warriors' Mounds | 16 | TRB → CON → WM | Five mounds in the Caspian foothills, unusual regularity, 1289; the traveler confirmed the mounds and refused the identification; the archive leaves the question open with him |
| KYA-17 | The Golden Basin | 17 | SAM → MRG → WM | The basin was used twice in the same ceremony; the court notation records both; what the object did was remember; the basin was melted; the notation was not |
| KYA-18 | Zerdusht's Tree | 18 | YAZ → MRG → WM | The tree was planted at a conversion and grew five centuries; forty wagons for the branches, three hundred for the trunk; the scholar who described it understood there was no longer a reason to plant another |
| KYA-19 | Maníjeh's Letter | 19 | NIS → RGS → WM | The form was invented by a woman stripped of everything except the fact of her condition; fourteen other women used it for legal petitions; the form survives because the condition it describes recurs |
| KYA-20 | The Dragon-Cart Design | 20 | TBZ → NIS → WM | The commission was precise and the engineering problem was solved; the guild's record ends at delivery; whether the dragon was killed is outside the document's scope |
| KYA-21 | The Kazú-Tree Arrow | 21 | SIS → NIS → WM | The Símúrgh specified the tree because the specificity was the point; the forester documented the matching tree; the branch and tag have been kept together since 1289; the archive holds the specification and the reluctance in the same cedar case |
| KYA-22 | The Pit Record | 22 | HRT → SAM → WM | The builder recorded dimensions, blade specifications, eleven days, and payment received in full; whether he knew what the pits were for is not recorded; the professional record ends at payment |
| KYA-23 | The Four Goblets | 23 | SAM → CON → WM | The archive cannot confirm whether the goblet is one of the four Alexander took from Dárá's table; the claim is intact; the verification is not possible; the archive holds both |
| KYA-24 | The Wall Stone | 24 | TRB → CON → WM | The wall is attested in two literary traditions; a merchant found a wall matching the description and reported it still standing in 1341; the archive holds one stone; the wall holds the rest |
| KYA-25 | Firdusi's Complaint | 25 | NIS → TBZ → WM | The satire is the part of the Shah-Nameh that Mahmúd did not receive; the anger was about the thirty years, not the silver; the archive holds the work and the complaint — that is what an archive should hold |
